# KITABU QUEST Grade 11 English

## Title Page

KITABU QUEST Grade 11 English

Kenya CBC content draft for review

Mascot: fox rabbit

This content-first draft was regenerated from Kitabu curriculum data using a topic-by-topic book plan. It is designed to help learners read, practise, discuss, create, and revise with confidence.

Cover artwork is intentionally deferred until all content has passed review and is marked ready-for-cover.

## How To Use This Book

This book helps Grade 11 learners study English through clear lessons, worked examples, activities, practice, and revision.

Use the inquiry questions to think first. Read the explanation. Try the guided example. Complete the activity. Answer the practice questions. Correct your work and ask for support where needed.

Read, speak, listen, write, and edit in connected language tasks.

Every unit has a local example, a misconception to avoid, success criteria, and a check for understanding.

## Learning Skills In This Book

Each chapter builds knowledge, skills, values, creativity, communication, collaboration, critical thinking, digital literacy, and self-confidence.

Look for these page types: Learn, Example, Activity, Practice, Reflection, Home Link, and Review.

How to study well: read the goal first, underline new words, try the example before reading the answer, discuss with a partner, and correct your work after feedback. Do not skip the reflection questions. They help you notice what you understand and what needs more practice.

## 1Extensive Listening for enjoyment and general information Identifying text features (Text features: listen to a text: Learn

Think of the listener or reader first. The task is connected to 1Extensive Listening for enjoyment and general information Identifying text features (Text features: listen to a text.

Important idea: 1Extensive Listening for enjoyment and general information Identifying text features (Text features: listen to a text.

Speaking and listening are active skills. Listen for the main point, take brief notes, respond respectfully, and support your own point with a reason or example. Clear speech uses pace, volume, eye contact, and appropriate tone.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

## 1Extensive Listening for enjoyment and general information Identifying text features (Text features: listen to a text: Worked Example

Listening model for 1Extensive Listening for enjoyment and general information Identifying text features (Text features: listen to a text: Catch key details from a recording or teacher-read text.

Transcript excerpt: "At dawn, Naliaka and her brother carried seedlings to the school garden. Their teacher reminded the club to water the young trees before the sun became hot. By Friday, every group had labelled its row and recorded the number of seedlings planted."

Question: Which details should a listener record?

Model answer:
1. Who: Naliaka, her brother, the teacher, and the club.
2. Where: the school garden.
3. What happened: seedlings were planted, watered, labelled, and recorded.
4. Message: careful listening helps us retell events in order.

Grade 11 check: A strong listening answer names people, place, event order, and message without inventing details.

## Life Lessons in Plays: reading fluency in communication: Worked Example

Reading model for Life Lessons in Plays: reading fluency in communication: Read the short passage, then answer with evidence.

Passage: Amina borrowed a library book about a school clean-water campaign. She first skimmed the headings, then read the middle paragraph slowly because it explained why learners labelled taps, reported leaks, and kept drinking cups clean. In her notebook, she wrote one main idea and two details from the paragraph instead of guessing from the title alone.

Question: What does Amina's behaviour show about careful reading?

Model answer:
1. Point: Amina reads carefully and uses evidence.
2. Evidence: She skims headings, rereads the paragraph, and records one main idea with two details.
3. Explanation: These actions show that a strong reading answer comes from the text, not from a quick guess.

Grade 11 check: A strong answer names the idea, quotes or paraphrases evidence, and explains why the evidence matters.

## Word Classes Nouns: (regular/ irregular: Worked Example

Language model for Word Classes Nouns: (regular/ irregular: Sort words by class before using them in sentences.

Word set: Nairobi, chair, water, courage, several, quickly.

Model classification:
1. Nairobi is a proper noun because it names a specific place.
2. Chair is a common count noun because we can count one chair, two chairs.
3. Water is a common non-count noun in ordinary use.
4. Courage is an abstract noun because it names a quality.
5. Several is a determiner because it tells about quantity.
6. Quickly is an adverb because it describes how an action is done.

Model sentence: Several learners quickly carried the chair from Nairobi Hall after the reading activity.

Explanation: Word classes help us choose correct forms and place words where the sentence makes sense.

Your turn: Classify four words linked to word classes nouns: (regular/ irregular, then use two of them in correct sentences.

## Word Classes Nouns: (regular/ irregular: Worked Example

Language model for Word Classes Nouns: (regular/ irregular: Sort words by class before using them in sentences.

Word set: Nairobi, chair, water, courage, several, quickly.

Model classification:
1. Nairobi is a proper noun because it names a specific place.
2. Chair is a common count noun because we can count one chair, two chairs.
3. Water is a common non-count noun in ordinary use.
4. Courage is an abstract noun because it names a quality.
5. Several is a determiner because it tells about quantity.
6. Quickly is an adverb because it describes how an action is done.

Model sentence: Several learners quickly carried the chair from Nairobi Hall after the reading activity.

Explanation: Word classes help us choose correct forms and place words where the sentence makes sense.

Your turn: Classify four words linked to word classes nouns: (regular/ irregular, then use two of them in correct sentences.

## Critical Listening Recognising a speaker's attitude Analysing the speaker's message (Context: Grade-appropriate: attitude of the speaker: Worked Example

Listening model for Critical Listening Recognising a speaker's attitude Analysing the speaker's message (Context: Grade-appropriate: attitude of the speaker: Catch key details from a recording or teacher-read text.

Transcript excerpt: "At dawn, Naliaka and her brother carried seedlings to the school garden. Their teacher reminded the club to water the young trees before the sun became hot. By Friday, every group had labelled its row and recorded the number of seedlings planted."

Question: Which details should a listener record?

Model answer:
1. Who: Naliaka, her brother, the teacher, and the club.
2. Where: the school garden.
3. What happened: seedlings were planted, watered, labelled, and recorded.
4. Message: careful listening helps us retell events in order.

Grade 11 check: A strong listening answer names people, place, event order, and message without inventing details.

Reading well means tracking meaning, not just finishing words. Identify the main idea, notice how details support it, and answer with evidence from the text. For literature, connect character, setting, plot, language, and theme.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

## Reading 2.2.1 Extensive: Worked Example

Reading model for Reading 2.2.1 Extensive: Read the short passage, then answer with evidence.

Passage: Amina borrowed a library book about a school clean-water campaign. She first skimmed the headings, then read the middle paragraph slowly because it explained why learners labelled taps, reported leaks, and kept drinking cups clean. In her notebook, she wrote one main idea and two details from the paragraph instead of guessing from the title alone.

Question: What does Amina's behaviour show about careful reading?

Model answer:
1. Point: Amina reads carefully and uses evidence.
2. Evidence: She skims headings, rereads the paragraph, and records one main idea with two details.
3. Explanation: These actions show that a strong reading answer comes from the text, not from a quick guess.

Grade 11 check: A strong answer names the idea, quotes or paraphrases evidence, and explains why the evidence matters.

## Word classes: Main and Auxiliary Verbs Primary auxiliaries used in Yes-No questions: primary and modal auxiliary: Worked Example

Language model for Word classes: Main and Auxiliary Verbs Primary auxiliaries used in Yes-No questions: primary and modal auxiliary: Make the verb agree with the subject and time.

Original sentence: The reading group discuss word classes: main and auxiliary verbs primary auxiliaries used in yes-no questions: primary and modal auxiliary yesterday and writes notes today.

Revision steps:
1. Find the subject: the reading group.
2. Match the time words: yesterday needs past tense; today needs present tense.
3. Keep each verb in the correct form.
4. Improved sentence: The reading group discussed word classes: main and auxiliary verbs primary auxiliaries used in yes-no questions: primary and modal auxiliary yesterday and writes notes today.

Explanation: Verb tense shows time, and agreement shows whether the subject is singular or plural.

Your turn: Write two correct sentences about word classes: main and auxiliary verbs primary auxiliaries used in yes-no questions: primary and modal auxiliary: one in the past tense and one in the present tense.

## Intensive Listening: speech Picking out synonyms and repeated words and phrases Types: select specific details: Worked Example

Listening model for Intensive Listening: speech Picking out synonyms and repeated words and phrases Types: select specific details: Catch key details from a recording or teacher-read text.

Transcript excerpt: "At dawn, Naliaka and her brother carried seedlings to the school garden. Their teacher reminded the club to water the young trees before the sun became hot. By Friday, every group had labelled its row and recorded the number of seedlings planted."

Question: Which details should a listener record?

Model answer:
1. Who: Naliaka, her brother, the teacher, and the club.
2. Where: the school garden.
3. What happened: seedlings were planted, watered, labelled, and recorded.
4. Message: careful listening helps us retell events in order.

Grade 11 check: A strong listening answer names people, place, event order, and message without inventing details.

## Intensive Listening: speech Picking out synonyms and repeated words and phrases Types: take notes from an oral text: Worked Example

Listening model for Intensive Listening: speech Picking out synonyms and repeated words and phrases Types: take notes from an oral text: Catch key details from a recording or teacher-read text.

Transcript excerpt: "At dawn, Naliaka and her brother carried seedlings to the school garden. Their teacher reminded the club to water the young trees before the sun became hot. By Friday, every group had labelled its row and recorded the number of seedlings planted."

Question: Which details should a listener record?

Model answer:
1. Who: Naliaka, her brother, the teacher, and the club.
2. Where: the school garden.
3. What happened: seedlings were planted, watered, labelled, and recorded.
4. Message: careful listening helps us retell events in order.

Grade 11 check: A strong listening answer names people, place, event order, and message without inventing details.

## Speaking Fluency: Public Speaking Different articulations of vowel letters 'ea' and 'ie': appropriate non-verbal cues during: Worked Example

Language model for Speaking Fluency: Public Speaking Different articulations of vowel letters 'ea' and 'ie': appropriate non-verbal cues during: Make the verb agree with the subject and time.

Original sentence: The reading group discuss speaking fluency: public speaking different articulations of vowel letters 'ea' and 'ie': appropriate non-verbal cues during yesterday and writes notes today.

Revision steps:
1. Find the subject: the reading group.
2. Match the time words: yesterday needs past tense; today needs present tense.
3. Keep each verb in the correct form.
4. Improved sentence: The reading group discussed speaking fluency: public speaking different articulations of vowel letters 'ea' and 'ie': appropriate non-verbal cues during yesterday and writes notes today.

Explanation: Verb tense shows time, and agreement shows whether the subject is singular or plural.

Your turn: Write two correct sentences about speaking fluency: public speaking different articulations of vowel letters 'ea' and 'ie': appropriate non-verbal cues during: one in the past tense and one in the present tense.

## Reading 3.2.1 Extensive: Worked Example

Reading model for Reading 3.2.1 Extensive: Read the short passage, then answer with evidence.

Passage: Amina borrowed a library book about a school clean-water campaign. She first skimmed the headings, then read the middle paragraph slowly because it explained why learners labelled taps, reported leaks, and kept drinking cups clean. In her notebook, she wrote one main idea and two details from the paragraph instead of guessing from the title alone.

Question: What does Amina's behaviour show about careful reading?

Model answer:
1. Point: Amina reads carefully and uses evidence.
2. Evidence: She skims headings, rereads the paragraph, and records one main idea with two details.
3. Explanation: These actions show that a strong reading answer comes from the text, not from a quick guess.

Grade 11 check: A strong answer names the idea, quotes or paraphrases evidence, and explains why the evidence matters.

## Word Classes: Position of adjectives: that can be used both attributively and: pick out adjectives: Worked Example

Language model for Word Classes: Position of adjectives: that can be used both attributively and: pick out adjectives: Sort words by class before using them in sentences.

Word set: Nairobi, chair, water, courage, several, quickly.

Model classification:
1. Nairobi is a proper noun because it names a specific place.
2. Chair is a common count noun because we can count one chair, two chairs.
3. Water is a common non-count noun in ordinary use.
4. Courage is an abstract noun because it names a quality.
5. Several is a determiner because it tells about quantity.
6. Quickly is an adverb because it describes how an action is done.

Model sentence: Several learners quickly carried the chair from Nairobi Hall after the reading activity.

Explanation: Word classes help us choose correct forms and place words where the sentence makes sense.

Your turn: Classify four words linked to word classes: position of adjectives: that can be used both attributively and: pick out adjectives, then use two of them in correct sentences.

## Writing 3.4.1 Paragraphing: Learn

Before answering, decide whether you are reading, speaking, listening, writing, or editing.

Important idea: Writing 3.4.1 Paragraphing.

Writing improves when purpose, audience, structure, and editing work together. Plan the main idea first, arrange details in a sensible order, choose exact words, and revise at least one sentence after feedback.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Success criteria:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

For Grade 11, use clear sentences, examples, and respectful discussion.

Inline visual support:

| Communication part | What to show |
| --- | --- |
| Purpose | The reading, listening, speaking, writing, or grammar task in Writing 3.4.1 Paragraphing. |
| Audience | Who needs to understand the message. |
| Evidence or example | A detail from the passage, conversation, notice, report, or spoken task. |
| Revision | One sentence improved for clarity, tone, spelling, punctuation, or grammar. |

Method for English: Read, speak, listen, write, and edit in connected language tasks.

Key words:
- Writing 3.4.1 Paragraphing
- Writing
- Paragraphing

Explain the idea in your own words before attempting the activities.

## Writing 3.4.1 Paragraphing: Worked Example

Writing model for Writing 3.4.1 Paragraphing: Plan before writing.

Task: Write a short response that explains a learning activity connected to this topic.

Plan:
1. Purpose: explain what happened and what was learned.
2. Audience: classmates, teacher, parents, or public readers.
3. Opening sentence: Our group studied writing 3.4.1 paragraphing by reading the task, discussing evidence, and improving one draft.
4. Three details: what learners did, what evidence was used, and what changed after feedback.
5. Closing sentence: The activity showed that clear planning and revision improve communication.

Model paragraph: Our group studied writing 3.4.1 paragraphing by reading the task, discussing evidence, and improving one draft. Each learner contributed one sentence, then the group checked word choice and punctuation. We revised the weakest sentence so the message became clearer. The activity showed that clear planning and revision improve communication.

Your turn: Use the same plan for a paragraph, report, letter, or speech linked to this topic.

## Writing 3.4.1 Paragraphing: Activity And Practice

Activity for Writing 3.4.1 Paragraphing: Complete the listening, reading, grammar, writing, or speaking task named in the outcomes below, then improve one answer after feedback.

Use this routine for every answer: identify the exact language skill, state the audience or purpose, use evidence or an example, organise the response, and edit one sentence.

1. Plan and write one short paragraph, letter part, report note, or speech opening with a clear audience, then edit one sentence for stronger meaning. Target outcome: describe the structure of a well-formed paragraph and the parts of an essay (introduction, body, conclusion).
2. Plan and write one short paragraph, letter part, report note, or speech opening with a clear audience, then edit one sentence for stronger meaning. Target outcome: use linking words that indicate result illustration, comparison, emphasis, comparison, emphasis for paragraph cohesion.
3. Write two correct sentences that use the grammar point, underline the key word or punctuation mark, and explain why it is correct. Target outcome: develop a well-structured paragraph (a topic sentence, supporting sentences and a clincher /concluding sentence) for cohesion.

Correction check:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

Reflection: What meaning became clearer after editing? Which sentence still needs work?

Home link: Ask someone at home for a short story, instruction, opinion, or example linked to writing 3.4.1 paragraphing. Write four clear sentences about it.

## Selective Listening for essential information Note-taking: specific details (such as, names, dates, numbers, facts): Lesson Opener

By the end of this lesson sequence, you should be able to:
- identify specific details (such as, names, dates, numbers, facts) from an audio recording with minimal distraction
- distinguish main ideas from supporting details in spoken texts
- take brief structured notes while listening to extract relevant information

Inquiry questions:

- How can one avoid distractions while listening?

## Selective Listening for essential information Note-taking: specific details (such as, names, dates, numbers, facts): Learn

Begin with a real language task: a class discussion, reading club, letter, report, speech, or story situation linked to Selective Listening for essential information Note-taking: specific details (such as, names, dates, numbers, facts).

Important idea: Selective Listening for essential information Note-taking: specific details (such as, names, dates, numbers, facts).

Speaking and listening are active skills. Listen for the main point, take brief notes, respond respectfully, and support your own point with a reason or example. Clear speech uses pace, volume, eye contact, and appropriate tone.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

## Selective Listening for essential information Note-taking: specific details (such as, names, dates, numbers, facts): Worked Example

Listening model for Selective Listening for essential information Note-taking: specific details (such as, names, dates, numbers, facts): Catch key details from a recording or teacher-read text.

Transcript excerpt: "At dawn, Naliaka and her brother carried seedlings to the school garden. Their teacher reminded the club to water the young trees before the sun became hot. By Friday, every group had labelled its row and recorded the number of seedlings planted."

Question: Which details should a listener record?

Model answer:
1. Who: Naliaka, her brother, the teacher, and the club.
2. Where: the school garden.
3. What happened: seedlings were planted, watered, labelled, and recorded.
4. Message: careful listening helps us retell events in order.

Grade 11 check: A strong listening answer names people, place, event order, and message without inventing details.

## Selective Listening for essential information Note-taking: specific details (such as, names, dates, numbers, facts): Activity And Practice

Activity for Selective Listening for essential information Note-taking: specific details (such as, names, dates, numbers, facts): Complete the listening, reading, grammar, writing, or speaking task named in the outcomes below, then improve one answer after feedback.

Use this routine for every answer: identify the exact language skill, state the audience or purpose, use evidence or an example, organise the response, and edit one sentence.

1. Listen to a short story, poem, dialogue, or teacher-read transcript, then write three key details: title or speaker, place/event, and message. Target outcome: identify specific details (such as, names, dates, numbers, facts) from an audio recording with minimal distraction.
2. Listen to a short story, poem, dialogue, or teacher-read transcript, then write three key details: title or speaker, place/event, and message. Target outcome: distinguish main ideas from supporting details in spoken texts.
3. Listen to a short story, poem, dialogue, or teacher-read transcript, then write three key details: title or speaker, place/event, and message. Target outcome: take brief structured notes while listening to extract relevant information.

Correction check:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

Reflection: What meaning became clearer after editing? Which sentence still needs work?

Original note: A school debate helps learners practise listening, organise arguments, respond to different views, and speak with confidence before an audience.

Good summary: A school debate builds communication by training learners to listen, organise ideas, respond respectfully, and speak confidently.

Steps:
1. Keep the main idea.
2. Remove repeated details.
3. Use your own sentence structure.
4. Preserve the meaning.

Your turn: Summarise a short paragraph linked to reading 4.2.1 study skills: summarise information from multiple sources. in one clear sentence.

3. Remove repeated words.

## Writing 4.4.1 Mechanics: explain the use of the target punctuation marks: Worked Example

Language model for Writing 4.4.1 Mechanics: explain the use of the target punctuation marks: Use punctuation to guide meaning.

Original sentence: before the debate chairperson said we must listen carefully

Revision steps:
1. Begin with a capital letter.
2. Add a comma after the opening time phrase where it helps the reader pause.
3. Put spoken words inside quotation marks if the sentence reports exact speech.
4. Improved sentence: Before the debate, the chairperson said, "We must listen carefully."

Explanation: Capital letters, commas, and quotation marks help the reader know where the sentence begins, pauses, and reports speech.

## Interactive Listening Confirming understanding, questioning, interpreting non-verbal cues: ways of confirming understanding information: Worked Example

Listening model for Interactive Listening Confirming understanding, questioning, interpreting non-verbal cues: ways of confirming understanding information: Catch key details from a recording or teacher-read text.

Transcript excerpt: "At dawn, Naliaka and her brother carried seedlings to the school garden. Their teacher reminded the club to water the young trees before the sun became hot. By Friday, every group had labelled its row and recorded the number of seedlings planted."

Question: Which details should a listener record?

Model answer:
1. Who: Naliaka, her brother, the teacher, and the club.
2. Where: the school garden.
3. What happened: seedlings were planted, watered, labelled, and recorded.
4. Message: careful listening helps us retell events in order.

Grade 11 check: A strong listening answer names people, place, event order, and message without inventing details.

## I / bit, /aı /- bite Online meetings, discussions, and interviews: pronounce words featuring the digraphs: Worked Example

Language model for I / bit, /aı /- bite Online meetings, discussions, and interviews: pronounce words featuring the digraphs: Sort words by class before using them in sentences.

Word set: Nairobi, chair, water, courage, several, quickly.

Model classification:
1. Nairobi is a proper noun because it names a specific place.
2. Chair is a common count noun because we can count one chair, two chairs.
3. Water is a common non-count noun in ordinary use.
4. Courage is an abstract noun because it names a quality.
5. Several is a determiner because it tells about quantity.
6. Quickly is an adverb because it describes how an action is done.

Model sentence: Several learners quickly carried the chair from Nairobi Hall after the reading activity.

Explanation: Word classes help us choose correct forms and place words where the sentence makes sense.

Your turn: Classify four words linked to i / bit, /aı /- bite online meetings, discussions, and interviews: pronounce words featuring the digraphs, then use two of them in correct sentences.

## Reading 5.2.1 Intensive: predictions about events and characters in a text: Worked Example

Reading model for Reading 5.2.1 Intensive: predictions about events and characters in a text: Read the short passage, then answer with evidence.

Passage: Amina borrowed a library book about a school clean-water campaign. She first skimmed the headings, then read the middle paragraph slowly because it explained why learners labelled taps, reported leaks, and kept drinking cups clean. In her notebook, she wrote one main idea and two details from the paragraph instead of guessing from the title alone.

Question: What does Amina's behaviour show about careful reading?

Model answer:
1. Point: Amina reads carefully and uses evidence.
2. Evidence: She skims headings, rereads the paragraph, and records one main idea with two details.
3. Explanation: These actions show that a strong reading answer comes from the text, not from a quick guess.

Grade 11 check: A strong answer names the idea, quotes or paraphrases evidence, and explains why the evidence matters.

## Word Classes: Adverbs of degree: intensifiers and moderating adverbs Adverb-forming suffixes -ly/-: distinguish between intensifiers: Worked Example

Language model for Word Classes: Adverbs of degree: intensifiers and moderating adverbs Adverb-forming suffixes -ly/-: distinguish between intensifiers: Make the verb agree with the subject and time.

Original sentence: The reading group discuss word classes: adverbs of degree: intensifiers and moderating adverbs adverb-forming suffixes -ly/-: distinguish between intensifiers yesterday and writes notes today.

Revision steps:
1. Find the subject: the reading group.
2. Match the time words: yesterday needs past tense; today needs present tense.
3. Keep each verb in the correct form.
4. Improved sentence: The reading group discussed word classes: adverbs of degree: intensifiers and moderating adverbs adverb-forming suffixes -ly/-: distinguish between intensifiers yesterday and writes notes today.

Explanation: Verb tense shows time, and agreement shows whether the subject is singular or plural.

## Responsive Listening Recognising/in terpreting speaker's message Avoiding putting words in the speaker's: feelings and barriers: Worked Example

Listening model for Responsive Listening Recognising/in terpreting speaker's message Avoiding putting words in the speaker's: feelings and barriers: Catch key details from a recording or teacher-read text.

Transcript excerpt: "At dawn, Naliaka and her brother carried seedlings to the school garden. Their teacher reminded the club to water the young trees before the sun became hot. By Friday, every group had labelled its row and recorded the number of seedlings planted."

Question: Which details should a listener record?

Model answer:
1. Who: Naliaka, her brother, the teacher, and the club.
2. Where: the school garden.
3. What happened: seedlings were planted, watered, labelled, and recorded.
4. Message: careful listening helps us retell events in order.

Grade 11 check: A strong listening answer names people, place, event order, and message without inventing details.

## ess (n) 'acc: pronounce words with the target double consonant clusters: Worked Example

Language model for ess (n) 'acc: pronounce words with the target double consonant clusters: Sort words by class before using them in sentences.

Word set: Nairobi, chair, water, courage, several, quickly.

Model classification:
1. Nairobi is a proper noun because it names a specific place.
2. Chair is a common count noun because we can count one chair, two chairs.
3. Water is a common non-count noun in ordinary use.
4. Courage is an abstract noun because it names a quality.
5. Several is a determiner because it tells about quantity.
6. Quickly is an adverb because it describes how an action is done.

Model sentence: Several learners quickly carried the chair from Nairobi Hall after the reading activity.

Explanation: Word classes help us choose correct forms and place words where the sentence makes sense.

Your turn: Classify four words linked to ess (n) 'acc: pronounce words with the target double consonant clusters, then use two of them in correct sentences.

## Reading 6.2.1 Extensive: Reading for Meaning and Evidence: Worked Example

Reading model for Reading 6.2.1 Extensive: Reading for Meaning and Evidence: Read the short passage, then answer with evidence.

Passage: Amina borrowed a library book about a school clean-water campaign. She first skimmed the headings, then read the middle paragraph slowly because it explained why learners labelled taps, reported leaks, and kept drinking cups clean. In her notebook, she wrote one main idea and two details from the paragraph instead of guessing from the title alone.

Question: What does Amina's behaviour show about careful reading?

Model answer:
1. Point: Amina reads carefully and uses evidence.
2. Evidence: She skims headings, rereads the paragraph, and records one main idea with two details.
3. Explanation: These actions show that a strong reading answer comes from the text, not from a quick guess.

Grade 11 check: A strong answer names the idea, quotes or paraphrases evidence, and explains why the evidence matters.

## Relative and Adverbial Clauses Relative clauses introduced by relative adverbs: (where, when: Worked Example

Language model for Relative and Adverbial Clauses Relative clauses introduced by relative adverbs: (where, when,: Make the verb agree with the subject and time.

Original sentence: The reading group discuss relative and adverbial clauses relative clauses introduced by relative adverbs: (where, when, yesterday and writes notes today.

Revision steps:
1. Find the subject: the reading group.
2. Match the time words: yesterday needs past tense; today needs present tense.
3. Keep each verb in the correct form.
4. Improved sentence: The reading group discussed relative and adverbial clauses relative clauses introduced by relative adverbs: (where, when, yesterday and writes notes today.

Explanation: Verb tense shows time, and agreement shows whether the subject is singular or plural.

## Writing 6.4.1 Creative: common idioms and fixed phrases in written: Worked Example

Writing model for Writing 6.4.1 Creative: common idioms and fixed phrases in written: Plan before writing.

Task: Write a short response that explains a learning activity connected to this topic.

3. Opening sentence: Our group studied writing 6.4.1 creative: common idioms and fixed phrases in written by reading the task, discussing evidence, and improving one draft.

5. Closing sentence: The activity showed that clear planning and revision improve communication.

Model paragraph: Our group studied writing 6.4.1 creative: common idioms and fixed phrases in written by reading the task, discussing evidence, and improving one draft. Each learner contributed one sentence, then the group checked word choice and punctuation. We revised the weakest sentence so the message became clearer. The activity showed that clear planning and revision improve communication.

## Writing 6.4.1 Creative: Recorded Stories, Poems, and Dialogues: Worked Example

Listening model for Writing 6.4.1 Creative: Recorded Stories, Poems, and Dialogues: Catch key details from a recording or teacher-read text.

Transcript excerpt: "At dawn, Naliaka and her brother carried seedlings to the school garden. Their teacher reminded the club to water the young trees before the sun became hot. By Friday, every group had labelled its row and recorded the number of seedlings planted."

Question: Which details should a listener record?

Model answer:
1. Who: Naliaka, her brother, the teacher, and the club.
2. Where: the school garden.
3. What happened: seedlings were planted, watered, labelled, and recorded.
4. Message: careful listening helps us retell events in order.

Grade 11 check: A strong listening answer names people, place, event order, and message without inventing details.

## Reading 7.2.1 Intensive: context clues in sentences and paragraphs to determine: Worked Example

Reading model for Reading 7.2.1 Intensive: context clues in sentences and paragraphs to determine: Read the short passage, then answer with evidence.

Passage: Amina borrowed a library book about a school clean-water campaign. She first skimmed the headings, then read the middle paragraph slowly because it explained why learners labelled taps, reported leaks, and kept drinking cups clean. In her notebook, she wrote one main idea and two details from the paragraph instead of guessing from the title alone.

Question: What does Amina's behaviour show about careful reading?

Model answer:
1. Point: Amina reads carefully and uses evidence.
2. Evidence: She skims headings, rereads the paragraph, and records one main idea with two details.
3. Explanation: These actions show that a strong reading answer comes from the text, not from a quick guess.

Grade 11 check: A strong answer names the idea, quotes or paraphrases evidence, and explains why the evidence matters.

## Noun clauses: Functions of Noun Clauses Subject: those that begin with whoever: Worked Example

Language model for Noun clauses: Functions of Noun Clauses Subject: those that begin with whoever: Make the verb agree with the subject and time.

Original sentence: The reading group discuss noun clauses: functions of noun clauses subject: those that begin with whoever yesterday and writes notes today.

Revision steps:
1. Find the subject: the reading group.
2. Match the time words: yesterday needs past tense; today needs present tense.
3. Keep each verb in the correct form.
4. Improved sentence: The reading group discussed noun clauses: functions of noun clauses subject: those that begin with whoever yesterday and writes notes today.

Explanation: Verb tense shows time, and agreement shows whether the subject is singular or plural.

Your turn: Write two correct sentences about noun clauses: functions of noun clauses subject: those that begin with whoever: one in the past tense and one in the present tense.

## Intensive Listening / Viewing Interpreting non-verbal cues Using semantic or meaning context: explain the non-verbal cues: Worked Example

Listening model for Intensive Listening / Viewing Interpreting non-verbal cues Using semantic or meaning context: explain the non-verbal cues: Catch key details from a recording or teacher-read text.

Transcript excerpt: "At dawn, Naliaka and her brother carried seedlings to the school garden. Their teacher reminded the club to water the young trees before the sun became hot. By Friday, every group had labelled its row and recorded the number of seedlings planted."

Question: Which details should a listener record?

Model answer:
1. Who: Naliaka, her brother, the teacher, and the club.
2. Where: the school garden.
3. What happened: seedlings were planted, watered, labelled, and recorded.
4. Message: careful listening helps us retell events in order.

Grade 11 check: A strong listening answer names people, place, event order, and message without inventing details.

Transcript excerpt: "At dawn, Naliaka and her brother carried seedlings to the school garden. Their teacher reminded the club to water the young trees before the sun became hot. By Friday, every group had labelled its row and recorded the number of seedlings planted."

Question: Which details should a listener record?

Model answer:
1. Who: Naliaka, her brother, the teacher, and the club.
2. Where: the school garden.
3. What happened: seedlings were planted, watered, labelled, and recorded.
4. Message: careful listening helps us retell events in order.

Grade 11 check: A strong listening answer names people, place, event order, and message without inventing details.

## Intensive Listening / Viewing Interpreting non-verbal cues Using semantic or meaning context: interpret and use: Activity And Practice

1. Listen to a short story, poem, dialogue, or teacher-read transcript, then write three key details: title or speaker, place/event, and message. Target outcome: interpret and use a variety of visuals for oral comprehension.
2. Listen to a short story, poem, dialogue, or teacher-read transcript, then write three key details: title or speaker, place/event, and message. Target outcome: appreciate non-verbal cues as elements in communication.

Correction check:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

Reflection: What meaning became clearer after editing? Which sentence still needs work?

## Writing 8.4.1 Functional: features of a simple survey report in functional: Worked Example

Media writing model for Writing 8.4.1 Functional: features of a simple survey report in functional: Turn facts into a short, lively article opening.

Task: Write the opening of a school news article about a clean-water campaign.

Weak opening: The campaign was good and many people came.

Improved opening: Learners at Baraka Secondary turned the assembly ground into a clean-water action centre on Friday, testing simple filters, interviewing health volunteers, and signing a pledge to report broken taps before water is wasted.

Why it works:
1. It names who, where, and when.
2. It uses active verbs: turned, testing, interviewing, signing.
3. It gives concrete details instead of saying only good.
4. It fits a public audience.

Your turn: Write a 60-word blog or newspaper opening for writing 8.4.1 functional: features of a simple survey report in functional using one fact, one vivid verb, and one clear audience.

## Writing 8.4.1 Functional: prepare an agenda, notice of meetings and minutes: Worked Example

Writing model for Writing 8.4.1 Functional: prepare an agenda, notice of meetings and minutes: Plan before writing.

Task: Write a short response that explains a learning activity connected to this topic.

Plan:

2. Audience: classmates, teacher, parents, or public readers.
3. Opening sentence: Our group studied writing 8.4.1 functional: prepare an agenda, notice of meetings and minutes by reading the task, discussing evidence, and improving one draft.
4. Three details: what learners did, what evidence was used, and what changed after feedback.
5. Closing sentence: The activity showed that clear planning and revision improve communication.

Model paragraph: Our group studied writing 8.4.1 functional: prepare an agenda, notice of meetings and minutes by reading the task, discussing evidence, and improving one draft. Each learner contributed one sentence, then the group checked word choice and punctuation. We revised the weakest sentence so the message became clearer. The activity showed that clear planning and revision improve communication.

## Selective Listening: Filtering and Extracting Information from Oral Texts: specific details in spoken announcements for effective: Worked Example

Listening model for Selective Listening: Filtering and Extracting Information from Oral Texts: specific details in spoken announcements for effective: Catch key details from a recording or teacher-read text.

Transcript excerpt: "At dawn, Naliaka and her brother carried seedlings to the school garden. Their teacher reminded the club to water the young trees before the sun became hot. By Friday, every group had labelled its row and recorded the number of seedlings planted."

Question: Which details should a listener record?

Model answer:
1. Who: Naliaka, her brother, the teacher, and the club.
2. Where: the school garden.
3. What happened: seedlings were planted, watered, labelled, and recorded.
4. Message: careful listening helps us retell events in order.

Grade 11 check: A strong listening answer names people, place, event order, and message without inventing details.

## Critical Reading: Distinguishin g facts from opinions (short text such as poem,: figurative expressions, irony, diction: Worked Example

Listening model for Critical Reading: Distinguishin g facts from opinions (short text such as poem,: figurative expressions, irony, diction,: Catch key details from a recording or teacher-read text.

Transcript excerpt: "At dawn, Naliaka and her brother carried seedlings to the school garden. Their teacher reminded the club to water the young trees before the sun became hot. By Friday, every group had labelled its row and recorded the number of seedlings planted."

Question: Which details should a listener record?

Model answer:
1. Who: Naliaka, her brother, the teacher, and the club.
2. Where: the school garden.
3. What happened: seedlings were planted, watered, labelled, and recorded.
4. Message: careful listening helps us retell events in order.

Grade 11 check: A strong listening answer names people, place, event order, and message without inventing details.

## Sentences Subject-verb agreement Question tags: outline the rules of subject-verb agreement in communication: Worked Example

Language model for Sentences Subject-verb agreement Question tags: outline the rules of subject-verb agreement in communication: Make the verb agree with the subject and time.

Original sentence: The reading group discuss sentences subject-verb agreement question tags: outline the rules of subject-verb agreement in communication yesterday and writes notes today.

Revision steps:
1. Find the subject: the reading group.
2. Match the time words: yesterday needs past tense; today needs present tense.
3. Keep each verb in the correct form.
4. Improved sentence: The reading group discussed sentences subject-verb agreement question tags: outline the rules of subject-verb agreement in communication yesterday and writes notes today.

Explanation: Verb tense shows time, and agreement shows whether the subject is singular or plural.

Your turn: Write two correct sentences about sentences subject-verb agreement question tags: outline the rules of subject-verb agreement in communication: one in the past tense and one in the present tense.

## Answer And Teacher Notes

A strong English answer should:

- match the purpose and audience;
- use evidence from the passage, discussion, task, or real context;
- organise ideas into clear sentences and paragraphs;
- choose words that fit the tone;
- edit spelling, punctuation, grammar, and meaning after feedback.

For reading questions, use point, evidence, and explanation. For writing tasks, plan before writing and revise at least one sentence before submitting.

Answer-key guide: reading answers need a point, a quoted or paraphrased detail, and an explanation. Grammar answers need the corrected sentence and the reason for the correction. Writing answers need purpose, audience, structure, and editing evidence. Oral tasks need a clear position, reason, example, and respectful close.

Rubric for self-checking:
- 4: The answer addresses the exact question, uses subject vocabulary correctly, gives evidence or working, and includes a useful check or correction.
- 3: The main answer is correct but one part needs stronger evidence, clearer working, better labels, or a fuller explanation.
- 2: Some understanding is shown, but the answer is incomplete, too general, or hard to follow.
- 1: The answer is guessed, off topic, or missing the evidence needed for the task.

Correction routine: reread the command word, underline the key data or evidence, improve one weak step, and write one sentence explaining why the final answer is reasonable.

## Final Project

Create a final project for English.

Your project must include:
1. A clear title.
2. A real-life problem or question.
3. Evidence from at least three lessons.
4. A drawing, table, map, chart, model, dialogue, performance plan, artwork note, or worked solution.
5. A short reflection explaining what you learned.

Present your project to a classmate, teacher, parent, or study group.
